### F-EW-996 — History of the manuscripts, and Gonzague's condition for publication
- Claim: One winter evening early in 1895 Thérèse told stories of her childhood. At Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur's prompting, Pauline, as prioress, ordered her to write her childhood memories for her sisters only. She wrote in free time and gave the notebook on 20 January 1896. When Mère de Gonzague was prioress again, Pauline persuaded her on 2 June 1897 to order the continuation; it was written in June 1897 at one go without erasures, amid constant interruptions. She stopped writing in early July 1897. The chapter written for Marie in the 1896 retreat is the third piece. After her death Mère de Gonzague agreed to publish the three texts only if they were altered so all seemed addressed to her. In April 1910 Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur restored the original; an authentic copy went to Rome; the 1914 edition restored the three-part distinction.
- When / where: 1895–1898; 1910; 1914
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: Mère Agnès, actor
- Source: PA 6, [500]–[501], S19 15 Jul 1915
- New to English readers: partial (known to specialists; the condition imposed by Gonzague is less known)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: She says the changes "did not change the substance". The 1910 editors' note shows the 1910 court was told of cuts and changes.
- Extraction ID: B-F149

- Claim: She wrote in snatches, in rare free moments, with no draft, straight off. It was meant only as a family souvenir for her sisters, which explains some childish details she would have avoided if she had foreseen publication. When she handed it to Agnès, then prioress, Agnès put it aside carelessly without reading it.

### F-EW-1000 — Céline was stunned to learn whom Thérèse disliked
- Claim: To help Céline with her own antipathies, Thérèse told her of the long violence she did herself over one sister. Céline was stunned, and more so when told the name: Thérèse was so kind to that sister that Céline would have taken her for her best friend. Thérèse told this only once the victory was complete. Her advice: when exasperated, pray for the person and ask God to reward her for giving you a chance to suffer.
- When / where: 1895–97, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Céline; an unnamed sister
- Witness: Céline — HEARD
- Source: PO 4, f. 365r–v and 381r, S30, S32

- Notes: Céline too withholds the name.

### F-EW-1012 — "Oh, how rich you are!": the lost safety pin
- Claim: When a safety pin was taken from Céline and she missed it, Thérèse said: how rich you are, you cannot be happy! People give fairly willingly, but few let what is theirs be taken. - Claim: During a free day, Thérèse met Thérèse de St Augustin looking very joyful and said she now knew what her death would be. The nun said: you'll certainly die of the chest. Thérèse answered: no — what happiness, my blood will flow. There was much talk then of massacres.

### F-EW-1040 — The mussel shell for tears
- Claim: The novice cried often, for nothing. Thérèse took a mussel shell from her painting table, held the girl's hands so she could not wipe her eyes, and caught the tears in it; the novice laughed. Thérèse allowed her to cry as much as she liked, but only into the shell. Eight days before Thérèse's death, seeing she had cried all evening, Thérèse asked "Was it in the shell?", made her promise to keep the rule, and allowed her to cry only the first days after her death.

### F-EW-1057 — The Oblation: Pauline said yes "without enthusiasm"
- Claim: Thérèse asked Pauline, as prioress, for leave to offer herself as a victim to merciful Love. Her face was lit up; she seemed "on fire with love". ### F-EW-1062 — Gonzague's note after the "wound of love" (June 1895)
- Claim: An oral tradition links a Gonzague note to the "wound of love" of 14 June 1895. She wrote she had happily been wrong — her "double novice" was not tired — and that she preferred "the wounds made to my daughter" to the "dart" of poor Mère Saint-Ephrem (a Providence sister). She mocked devotions that tire even to hear of, praised the gaiety of St Teresa ("long live her tambourine"), and ended: "Let us be saints, but not affected ones by false devotion."

### F-EW-1064 — 9 June 1895: she led Céline in silence to Mère Agnès
- Claim: On Trinity Sunday, 9 June 1895, during Mass, she felt pressed to offer herself as a holocaust victim to merciful Love. After Mass she drew Céline after her in search of the prioress, seeming beside herself and not speaking. Finding Mère Agnès, she asked permission to offer herself with Céline, with a short explanation. Agnès was in a hurry, did not seem to understand much, and allowed everything, trusting her discretion. Thérèse then composed the Act, which she always carried on her heart.

### F-EW-1092 — Trial of faith: "if you spent five minutes in it"; a confessor who made it worse; the Creed in blood
- Claim: She told no one of her temptation against the existence of heaven, for fear of passing it on. Sometimes with Céline she let slip: "If you knew... if you spent only five minutes in the trials I endure!" She spoke of it to confessors; one increased her trouble by calling her state very dangerous. ### F-EW-1093 — Over 500 "points of fire" — Céline counted them
- Claim: She went to the laundry burning with fever, and to the drying-yard, with back or chest torn by unhealed blistering plasters. After one doctor's session in which more than 500 points of fire (cauterisation) were made on her side — Céline counted them — she climbed to her cell and rested on her hard straw mattress, because at that time sick nuns not yet in the infirmary were given no mattress.
- When / where: 1896–97, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; the doctor (unnamed); Céline
- Witness: Céline — SAW and counted
- Source: PO 4, f. 380r–v, S32
- Original words: « plus de 500 pointes de feu sur le côté (c'est moi que les [ai] comptées) »

- Notes: Treatment: vesicatories and pointes de feu.

### F-EW-1096 — Flowers for the crucifix; "I am unnailing Him"
- Claim: She often caressed her crucifix with flowers; she tied a small flower to it and replaced it as soon as it wilted, never tolerating a faded one. When healthy, throwing roses at the cloister-yard crucifix, she carefully picked the petals so as to strew only fresh ones. Céline once saw her touching the crown of thorns and nails and asked what she was doing; surprised, she said: "I'm unnailing him... and taking off his crown of thorns."
- When / where: 1896–97
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline — SAW and HEARD

- Claim: In the linen room, Thérèse asked to be the aide of a sister whose character drove everyone away; this sister had "dark ideas" and did almost nothing. Marie names her as Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph. Thérèse told Marie she was to be pitied and forgiven; it was not her fault she was poorly gifted.

- Claim: In 1896 Mother Agnès and Sr Geneviève almost left for the Saigon Carmel. Thérèse told Marie this was very painful for her, because she was sure it was not God's will. Yet she did not say one word to turn them from it.

### F-EW-1101 — "That is why I look all golden"

### F-EW-1102 — Opposition: neglect at the start of her last illness
- Claim: Opposition: At the start of her last illness she was not treated with enough care, because she never complained. She was left to say the Office until her strength was gone; left without a mattress in her cell after blistering plasters and cauterising "points of fire"; not dispensed from common work, laundry and cleaning until the last extremity; she hung out washing with back and chest torn by fresh blisters.

- People: Thérèse; the prioress (Mother Marie de Gonzague)

### F-EW-1103 — The trial of faith: heaven, the chaplain, the Creed in blood
- Claim: For two years before her death she had a frightful temptation against faith, aimed especially at the existence of heaven. She spoke of it to no one for fear of passing it on; she was a little more explicit with Mother Agnès, but only in unfinished sentences. The chaplain nearly upset her by telling her "her state was very dangerous". She consulted Fr Godefroy Madelaine, Céline believes, or another extraordinary confessor, and on their advice wrote the Creed in her blood on the last page of the Gospel book she always carried on her heart. She told Céline she made very many acts of faith.
- When / where: Carmel, Easter 1896 – September 1897
- People: Thérèse; Mother Agnès; the chaplain (Abbé Youf); Fr Godefroy Madelaine (uncertain)
- Witness: Céline, partly heard from Thérèse
- Source: PA 8, [653]–[654], Session 28; again [664], [721]–[722]
- Original words: « son état était très dangereux »
- Translation: "her state was very dangerous"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the Creed and the chaplain's remark; medium for which confessor
- Notes: Opposition element: the chaplain's alarm. Céline says her desire for heaven was "tempered" by "terrible doubts about the existence of another life" [788].

- Claim: As sacristan, Thérèse told MT of her joy at touching the sacred vessels "like priests". She kissed them and made MT kiss the large host that was to be consecrated. In PA she says she was preparing "like Mary, the swaddling clothes of the Child Jesus" (the linens).

### F-EW-1114 — Her tears won a change in the communion custom
- Claim: On a communion day, when Thérèse was very ill (PA: very tired), the prioress ordered her to take a remedy before Mass. The house custom was that this meant losing communion. Thérèse burst into tears and pleaded so well that she could take the remedy after Mass. From that day the custom of losing communion in such cases was abolished.
- When / where: undated, probably 1896–1897 (PA names Gonzague as prioress)
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague

### F-EW-1115 — Rule on illness: say it once, then leave it to God
- Claim: Thérèse told MT that when ill she should simply tell the prioress and then leave it to God, whether cared for or not. As aide-infirmarian MT saw Thérèse follow this herself. She never said she suffered unless made to, and nothing cost her more than being fussed over.

### F-EW-1116 — Gonzague refused to excuse her from Matins
- Claim: Opposition: When MT, without Thérèse knowing, asked Gonzague to let Thérèse rest during Matins, Gonzague pushed her away. She said young people coddled their ailments, and in her day no one missed Matins. If Thérèse could not go on, she should come and say so herself.

- People: MT; Mère Marie de Gonzague

- Claim: Thérèse told MT that during the Office her heart often failed her from the effort of chanting and standing. She shook off the fatigue with the words "If I die, they'll see it."

- Claim: As prioress Gonzague imposed many small rules that she dropped or changed "by whim", so the community paid them little heed. Thérèse kept them all scrupulously and made MT do the same.

- Claim: The chaplain, Abbé Youf, suffered from "cerebral anaemia" and could not bear to give any direction outside confession. MT had no confidence in Gonzague. So she opened a trouble about purity to Thérèse.

### F-EW-1120 — The profession fight of early 1896
- Claim: Opposition: Before leaving office in 1896, Mère Agnès planned to receive the professions of Sr Geneviève and MT, whose novitiate time was over; the ecclesiastical superior also wanted this. Gonzague, novice mistress and expecting re-election, wanted to receive them herself. She opposed the plan violently before the whole community, in words very wounding to Agnès. MT's profession was pushed back until after the election.
- When / where: early 1896 (before the 21 Mar 1896 election), chapter/community

### F-EW-1121 — Thérèse's open protest against Gonzague
- Claim: Thérèse could not hold back and protested openly, before about fifteen nuns, against this "glaring injustice" by Gonzague. She said Gonzague was completely in the wrong and her conduct toward her prioress scandalous. A nun replied that the novice mistress had the right to humble her novices. Thérèse answered: no, this was not a humiliation one may impose.

- Witness: MT, present

- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — the tribunal asked for her exact words and the cause
- Notes: Only in PA; not in MT's PO. Very rare scene of Thérèse openly criticizing a superior. See E-S08, E-Q07.
- Extraction ID: E-F87
### F-EW-1122 — "I sing what I want to believe"
- Claim: Thérèse told MT of the darkness in her soul. MT, surprised, said her luminous poems contradicted this. Thérèse said: "I sing what I want to believe, but without any feeling." She would not say how black the night was, for fear MT would share her temptations. MT would never have guessed it from her words and actions.
- When / where: 1896–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, heard it
- Source: PA 21, p. 1219, Session 61

- Translation: I sing what I want to believe, but it is without any feeling.

- Claim: Distinctive: MT wished Thérèse would die during her thanksgiving after communion. Thérèse said quickly: no, that would be an extraordinary grace that would discourage "little souls" who could not imitate it. PA adds: "They must be able to imitate everything in me."

### F-EW-1124 — Each soul by its own road; the "skittles player" letter
- Claim: Thérèse would never force a soul onto her own way unless it was drawn to it. MT, "very childish in character", practised virtue by playing spiritual games with the Child Jesus. Thérèse went along with this and on Christmas night 1896 wrote her the letter to "my little spouse, skittles player on the mountain of Carmel". MT warns that some have wrongly taken this letter as Thérèse's general teaching, a "puerile spirituality", when it was written only to suit one soul.

- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, recipient

### F-EW-1125 — "I will not rest until the last of the elect is saved"
- Claim: Looking at the sky, MT said how happy they would be up there. Thérèse said she did not want heaven to rest: she wanted to spend her heaven doing good on earth until the end of the world, and would rest only after that. If she did not believe this, she would prefer to live until the end of time to save more souls. MT says she looked "inspired and full of certainty".
- When / where: undated, 1896–1897
- People: Thérèse; MT
- Witness: MT, heard it

- New to English readers: partial (famous line; the setting is new)

### F-EW-1126 — She kept Thérèse's hair as future relics
- Claim: From early 1896, when she saw Thérèse ill, MT began to collect souvenirs, especially her hair. Having permission to cut Thérèse's hair, she kept it instead of burning it, convinced it would work miracles after her death. She also wrote to her family that such perfection surprised her and her "holy little companion" would soon fly to heaven.

- People: MT; Thérèse
- Witness: MT, her own act
- Source: PO 17, f. 1095v; PA 21, p. 1285

### F-EW-1127 — Treatment: horsehair rubs and 500 "pointes de feu"
- Claim: After the two haemoptyses of 1896, the doctors La Néele and de Cornière said there was nothing serious, at least for now. They ordered frictions and "pointes de feu" (cauterization). Every morning Sr Geneviève rubbed her whole body with her horsehair belt; this exhausted her and left her aching all day. The doctor once applied up to 500 cautery points in one session. She did not complain and kept all community exercises until June 1897.
- When / where: 1896–1897
- People: Thérèse; Dr Francis La Néele; Dr Alexandre de Cornière; Sr Geneviève
- Witness: MT, aide-infirmarian until June 1897
- Source: PA 21, p. 1276, Session 64, 27 Sept 1916
- Original words: « le docteur lui en fit jusqu'à 500 en une fois »
- Translation: the doctor gave her as many as 500 in one session
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — MT was in the infirmary service then
- Notes: The horsehair ("ceinture de crin") detail is unusual; other witnesses may call it a horsehair glove.
- Extraction ID: E-F127

- Claim: Thérèse confided to TSA, who was "strangely surprised": "If you knew what darkness I am plunged in. I do not believe in eternal life; it seems to me that after this mortal life there is nothing; everything has vanished for me; only love remains." She spoke of this as a temptation.

### F-EW-1178 — The prioress was shut out of the chapter for Céline's profession vote
- Claim: Early 1896, hoping soon to replace Mère Agnès, Mère de Gonzague tried to delay the professions of Sr Geneviève (Céline) and Sr Marie de la Trinité so she could receive them herself. The superior, Canon Maupas, said aloud that the prioress must present both. Mère de Gonzague went pale, then told her party she would allow Céline's profession but formally opposed Marie de la Trinité's, though the latter had two months more of novitiate. Using the rule that a sister may not vote for her sister, she put the prioress (Agnès) out of the chapter, presided over the three sessions herself, and at the end left Agnès standing at the back with the novices and lay sisters.
- When / where: January–February 1896, Lisieux Carmel chapter

### F-EW-1179 — The only time she openly blamed Mère de Gonzague: Céline and the Saigon plan
- Claim: In January 1896 Pauline was prioress until the elections; Céline could be professed on 6 February. Mère de Gonzague, novice mistress and hoping to be elected, wanted to delay it. She was very displeased, said she would not vote for the novice, and began a campaign among the voting nuns to have Sr Geneviève sent to the Saigon Carmel, which wanted subjects. At a recreation without her, some sisters showed their ill will toward "the four sisters", as the Martins were called with contempt in such moments. One said the mistress had every right to test this novice like any other. Thérèse said with some emotion: "There are forms of trial that should not be used."

### F-EW-2532 — "The little toy of Jesus": the letter to Léonie of April 1895
- Claim: Léonie quoted a letter of April 1895 in which Thérèse said she had long liked to call herself "the little toy of Jesus". She had then thought that the Child Jesus had other souls, full of sublime virtues, who called themselves his "beautiful toys", and hers was only a "little toy" without value. To console herself she thought that children often enjoy "little toys" that they can leave or take, break or kiss as they wish, more than costly ones they hardly dare touch. She was glad to be poor, and wished to be more so each day, so that Jesus would take more pleasure in playing with her.

- Claim: The circular says that on the evening of her entry Thérèse had Marie sing verses expressing her life: that she could now 'sacrifice herself in silence, wait for heaven in peace, exposed to the rays of the divine Host, consume herself at that hearth of love, and love like a seraph'. Thérèse (called 'the old dean of the novitiate' after eight years) wrote to Jeanne that this 'gentle novice who delights the whole community' had all the qualities to become a saint; her beautiful voice made the community's joy, and gravity often failed Thérèse in her presence.

### F-EW-2577 — Marie's poverty: pins found under her feet, and her 'blazon' (a cross and the name of Jesus)
- Claim: The circular quotes a letter of Marie to her father: she wrote on the first white sheet at hand, sure it would be worth more to him than the perfumed letters of great ladies marked with a coat of arms; 'my blazon is too beautiful, too heavenly to be seen on earth; a cross and the name of Jesus, that is my blazon'. She said poverty had a special attraction for her. Her pincushion, given at her entry, had long been empty; she preferred to beg from others and best of all wait for God to send pins. When she had none she asked him and 'almost at once' found pins under her feet. 'The thought of becoming a saint never leaves me; it would be sad to see a child of so holy a family dishonour it.'
- When / where: 1896–1904, Lisieux Carmel (letter to Isidore Guérin)

### F-EW-2578 — Isidore Guérin's letters to his Carmelite daughter
- Claim: The circular quotes Isidore Guérin: on her entry (15 Aug 1895) 'we can now die, since we leave behind us a burning lamp that will never cease to burn before the divine Eucharist'. In a later letter he says her mother's death and his own loss had been 'cauterised' by God and that 'the regret is rather a calm and sweet happiness and a sort of pride'. He calls her 'my Benjamin' and 'the living portrait of your mother'. On 14 Apr 1905 he wrote his last letter to her: 'MY BELOVED LITTLE ANGEL, I bless you and thank you for all the happiness you gave me... I gave you my last kiss on his adorable Heart.' The mother's letter on the evening of her profession (25 Mar 1897) wishes that 'the last day of my dear child on this poor earth be as full of the love of God as that of her profession'.

- People: Isidore Guérin; Céline Guérin; Marie

### F-EW-2607 — 15 Aug 1895: Marie's entry, and what Thérèse laid on her bed
- Claim: Piat says Marie entered Carmel on 15 Aug 1895, her feast day. The letters of her parents to her (see the circular cards) followed. Even Jeanne, long reluctant, wrote that she felt God present among them. In her cell Marie found on the bed, among flowers, Thérèse's poem 'Vivre d'Amour', recently composed, and a piece written for her by Thérèse, 'Cantique d'une âme ayant trouvé le lieu de son repos' (to the tune of 'Mignon, connais-tu le pays?'; theme from Psalm 115, 'You have broken my bonds'), which she sang before the community at evening recreation. The name 'Marie de l'Eucharistie' had been given her at the parlour by Pauline. To her old family maid Marcelline Husé (now a Benedictine at Bayeux) who signed herself 'servant', Marie wrote: 'The word servant wounds my heart; you are my beloved little sister; we are both spouses of Jesus.'
- When / where: 15 Aug 1895, Lisieux Carmel

Levasseur, curé of Navarre near Évreux, her lay spiritual director. Mère Agnès gave her the habit and told her to 'consider yourself always the last of the house, the little servant of all'. Four days later, on Saturday 21 Mar 1896, Agnès's first three-year term expired. It took seven ballots to give Marie de Gonzague a majority; she left 'somewhat ulcerated', kept the novitiate for herself and confirmed Thérèse as sub-mistress, whose virtue impressed her.

### F-EW-2609 — Thérèse as sub-mistress of Marie: 'I am only your cousin' — 'No, you are my true little sister'
- Claim: Piat says that after Marie's entry Thérèse was Gonzague's 'sub-mistress' and Marie stayed in her direct contact. Thérèse, embracing her, replied with a hurt look: 'Oh, do not say that; you are my true little sister. If you knew how I love you!' Piat says Thérèse taught by small facts and remarks, not lectures: 'a mixture of simplicity and authority, familiarity and greatness that inspired both attraction and reverent fear.' Thérèse gave her for her anniversary of entry (15 Aug 1896) the poem 'Jésus seul' ('Jesus alone'), speaking in Marie's voice.

### F-EW-2610 — Thérèse's teaching to Marie: 'Think less of yourself, think of loving God' and 'Do not wait; begin now'
- Claim: Piat quotes what Marie recorded of Thérèse's advice: 'Please occupy yourself a little less with yourself; occupy yourself with loving God and let go of yourself. All your scruples are so many searches for yourself. Your griefs and pains all turn around the same pivot. Forget yourself, think of saving souls.' Marie, alarmed by the call to sanctity, said with mock grace: 'I promise to be a saint when you have gone to Heaven; then I will set about it with all my heart.' Thérèse replied: 'Oh, do not wait for that. Begin now. The month before my entry into Carmel remains a sweet memory. At first I told myself as you do: I will be holy when I am in Carmel; meanwhile I will not go out of my way... But God showed me the value of time. I did the contrary of what I planned; I wanted to prepare by being very faithful, and it was one of the most beautiful months of my life. Believe me, never wait until tomorrow to begin to become holy.'

### F-EW-2611 — Humility lessons: apologise seriously, apologise even when you were right

Another time, when Marie said lightly 'Well, I'll go ask pardon', Thérèse answered: 'Yes, you will go again asking pardon laughing. When you go to ask pardon you must do it humbly, in a serious way, not laughing.' ### F-EW-2614 — Recreation as a community exercise: 'be gay by virtue, not by caprice'
- Claim: Thérèse told Marie: 'Why do you go to recreation? To please yourself and find enjoyment? You must go as to another community exercise, out of fidelity, without stopping on the way. When you are sad, forget yourself and show gaiety... You should make the sacrifice of not sitting next to those you love.' Marie protested: 'Must one always restrain oneself so?' 'Yes, always you should deprive yourself. It is good to be gay in recreation, but there is a religious way of being gay. You are sometimes madly gay, you think it pleases the sisters. They laugh at your folly, it is true, but it does not edify them... Oblige the old ones by fetching their chairs... A little novice should always do too much. It would be so pretty!'

### F-EW-2615 — Marie's profession, 25 March 1897: Thérèse's song 'Mes armes' and the two children ransomed
- Claim: After a year of effort, broken by the community retreat of Oct 1896 preached by Père Godefroy Madelaine (Prior of the Premonstratensians of Mondaye), Marie made profession on Thursday 25 Mar 1897 in the chapter room, without the family present. The profession song sung that evening was written by Thérèse herself, expressing what she would have wanted to say to Céline had Sr Marie des Anges not written Céline's profession song. It is 'Mes armes', on the tune of Gounod's 'Chant du départ des missionnaires' for the Rue du Bac Society (Missions Étrangères), a review of poverty, chastity and obedience as the spiritual arms of the Bride of Christ. Marie asked her father to give to the anti-slavery work of Mgr de la Passardière the sum needed to redeem two black children: a boy to be named Joseph-Marie-Isidore and a girl Marie-Céline.

### F-EW-2667 — Sr Saint-Stanislas as first infirmarian in Thérèse's illness (April–Sept 1897)
- Claim: At the 1896 elections the posts were swapped: Sr Saint-Stanislas became first infirmarian and Thérèse went back to the sacristy. Thérèse soon fell ill and the old nun looked after her 'little girl' more from spring 1897. In April–May 1897, when blisters and fire-points (cautery) tore her back, Thérèse was moved: 'She dresses the wounds so gently! I see her choose the finest linens and apply them with a hand of velvet' (Circular). She said the infirmarian is 'there watching that you lack nothing. Ah! why do we not do the same for the spiritual illnesses of our sisters' (DE 393). She laughed at the 'snail syrup' (CJ 6.6.6), was moved to tears by a cooling drink brought one feverish evening (DE 785), said her mind was sometimes 'a little paralysed' by the infirmarian's constant activity ('two thousand steps where twenty would do') while she wrote her last manuscript in the chestnut walk (Ms C 17r); and to spare her deaf old nurse she put up with a heap of blankets in August (DE 520). She thanked her with 'smiles and caresses for the slightest service' (CJ 4.9.1).
- When / where: 1896–Sep 1897, Lisieux Carmel infirmary
- People: Sr Saint-Stanislas; Thérèse
- Witness: Carmel biography (modern), citing the Yellow Notebook (CJ) and Derniers Entretiens (DE)

### F-LF-220 — The Joan of Arc play and the photograph, January 1895
- Claim: Thérèse wrote and acted in the "recréation pieuse" RP 3 on Joan of Arc, performed 21 Jan 1895. Céline took the photographs in the sacristy courtyard between that date and 25 Mar 1895, with Thérèse still in costume. Photo no. 14 shows Thérèse as Joan in prison, visited by a saint played by Céline. Mère Agnès retouched it. The Carmel photos page names the visiting saint as St Margaret; other Carmel pages and *Le Normand* call it St Catherine. Laveille adds (fn.) that a tribunal set up by Mgr Lemonnier examined the negatives in the Carmel archive and in 1915 declared that the bust portrait used as the frontispiece of the large edition of the autobiography gives the best idea of her expression, being a careful composite of the photographs.

### F-LF-221 — The Act of Oblation to Merciful Love, 9 June 1895
- Claim: Thérèse pronounced her Act on the feast of the Holy Trinity, 9 Jun 1895, "as victim of holocaust to the Merciful Love of the Good God". The thought came during Mass: "shall Thy Justice alone receive victims of holocaust? Has not Thy merciful Love itself need of them?" After thanksgiving she asked Mère Agnès, "her face aglow", for leave. Mère Agnès agreed "without however appearing to attach special importance". She wrote the formula and wanted a theologian to check it. Fr Lemonnier of the Missionaries of La Délivrande, preaching a retreat, found it sound and changed one word: she had written "Infinite are the desires…", and on his advice she put "Immense". He thought two requests daring (the stigmata shining in her glorified body; Christ's presence kept between Communions) but did not cut them. She signed it "Marie-Françoise-Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus et de la Sainte Face, Rel. Carm. Ind." The Act was found after her death in a Gospel book she carried near her heart. She told only two novices and renewed it until her last hour. A few days later, at the Way of the Cross, she felt "wounded with a dart of fire so ardent that I thought I should die"; she told Mère Agnès at once, who paid no heed, and only on 7 Jul 1897 did Mère Agnès question her again (HA ch. xii p.228).
- When / where: 9 Jun 1895; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Fr Lemonnier
- Source: LAV p.355–363 (Summarium 1919 p.552 §1526)
- Source type: own-writing / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The full text is in LAV p.356–358 (own-writings family). Baseline says 9–11 June; Laveille gives 9 June.
### F-LF-222 — The first haemorrhage, night of 2–3 April 1896
- Claim: Laveille dates the first haemoptysis to the night between Holy Thursday and Good Friday, 3 Apr 1896. Thérèse related it in 1897: at midnight, having been refused leave to stay all night at the repository, she went to the cell. "No sooner did my head rest on the pillow, than I felt a burning stream rise to my lips; I believed that I was going to die, and my heart was thrilled with joy." She had just put out the small lamp, so she waited until morning and slept. At five she found her handkerchief "all blood-stained". She went to Prime and Chapter, told Mère Agnès her joy, felt no fatigue, and obtained leave to finish Lent. On Good Friday she kept all the austerities. That night a second haemorrhage came. She later told Mère Agnès (30 May 1897) about it (Carmel).
- When / where: night of 2–3 Apr 1896; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Marie de Gonzague
- Source: LAV p.365–367 (HA ch. ix p.156ff); Carmel page "la fabrication de l'Histoire d'une âme" (30 May 1897)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: "tuberculosis from Good Friday 1896")
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The blood was her sisters' first sign only later; she hid it. Laveille says Marie de Gonzague, "deceived by Thérèse's energy", did not think it serious.
### F-LF-223 — Treatments: massage, blistering, cupping, cauterizing
- Claim: On Good Friday 1896 the prioress (Marie de Gonzague, recently re-elected) let her keep all austerities; Thérèse even cleaned the convent windows between the long Offices. A novice found her "pale as death and almost fainting" after fasting on bread and water. She hid her state from her three sisters. Some weeks later they heard "the dry persistent cough". The prioress removed her from the sacristy. Dr de Cornière, the community doctor, and Dr La Néele (husband of cousin Jeanne) had noticed nothing alarming, and prescribed "massage, blistering, cupping, cauterizing". She endured them smiling. Céline testified that one day she counted five hundred applications of the cauterizing needle, and then folded a large blanket in four for a mattress, as she had no permission to give one (Summarium 1919 p.394 §1001). After Lent 1897 the doctor applied the needle more often. Dr de Cornière said: "I shall not be able to cure her… besides, this soul is not made for earth." A community doctor said that of a hundred persons attacked as she was, "perhaps two at most might have a chance of recovery".
- When / where: Apr 1896 – Jul 1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Dr de Cornière; Dr Francis La Néele; Céline; Marie de Gonzague
- Source: LAV p.311, p.350, p.370–372
- Source type: early-life / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Medical facts: the cauterizing needle (pointes de feu) was the standard 19th-century treatment for tuberculosis; the treatment named is as Laveille lists it. He does not use the word "tuberculosis" on these pages.
### F-LF-224 — Dream of Anne of Jesus, 10 May 1896
- Claim: Thérèse wrote of a dream on the night of 10 May 1896: at dawn she was in a gallery with her prioress, and three Carmelites in white mantles came from heaven. The tallest raised her veil and covered Thérèse with it; Thérèse knew her as Venerable Anne of Jesus, the foundress of Carmel in France. She asked if God would leave her long on earth and the answer was "Yes, soon… I promise you." She asked if God required only her small acts; Anne said God was "satisfied, well satisfied". Laveille's footnote: Anne of Jesus, companion of St Teresa of Ávila, died in 1621; her cause was introduced under Leo XIII; Thérèse admitted she had rarely thought of her before.
- When / where: 10 May 1896; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Anne of Jesus; Teresa of Ávila
- Source: LAV p.364–365 (HA ch. xii p.211)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for her account
- Notes: Connection to other saints: Anne of Jesus; Teresa of Ávila. Mère Agnès recorded also: "From my childhood, I had a strong presentiment that I should die young" (Summarium 1919 p.889 §2574).
### F-LF-225 — Léo Taxil, "Diana Vaughan", and the play of 21 June 1896
- Claim: Léo Taxil (Gabriel-Antoine Jogand-Pagès, b. Marseille 21 Mar 1854, d. Sceaux 31 Mar 1907), an anticlerical writer, staged a "conversion" in 1885 and was received by Leo XIII. From 1895 he published under the name of "Diana Vaughan", a supposed American priestess of "Palladism" who converted through Joan of Arc; the review ran from 21 Mar 1895 and the monthly *Mémoires d'une ex-palladiste* from 1 Jul 1895 to April 1897. Cardinal Parocchi's letters of 29 Nov and 16 Dec 1895 praised the work. In the Carmel, Marie Guérin wrote on 17 Jun 1896 that "Diana Vaughan" gave the novices the idea of a play, and Marie wrote on 21 Jun 1896 that Marie Guérin played St Michael fighting Satan behind a screen with forks, chains and thunder; Asmodeus spoke of Diana Vaughan; the piece, "Le Triomphe de l'humilité" (RP 7), was by Sr Thérèse, for the prioress's feast (21 Jun, feast of St Aloysius, Marie de Gonzague's baptismal name Rosalie in the notes). Thérèse believed Diana existed and wrote to her.
- When / where: 1895–21 Jun 1896; Paris; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Léo Taxil; "Diana Vaughan"; Marie Guérin; Marie du Sacré-Cœur; Thérèse; Card. Parocchi
- Source: web-context [T2]; Carmel letters https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie-a-mme-guerin-17-juin-1896/ and https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/de-soeur-marie-du-s-coeur-a-m-et-mme-guerin-21-juin-1896/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/archive/le-triomphe-de-lhumilite/ ; FR Wikipedia "Diana Vaughan"
- Source type: eyewitness (letters) / scholarship
- Rights: unknown / facts-only
- New to English readers: yes (baseline: "Taxil hoax not in baseline")
- Confidence: high for the letters
- Notes: Scholarly edition: Cerf 1975, "Le Triomphe de l'humilité… Thérèse mystifiée". The link between the Carmel's Joan of Arc photo and Taxil is in F-LF-226.
### F-LF-226 — The photo sent to "Diana", and the press conference of 19 April 1897
- Claim: Mère Agnès suggested that Thérèse send photo no. 14 (Joan in prison) to Diana in summer 1896, after retouching; "Diana" replied. From August 1896 doubts rose: a German Jesuit called the texts fabrication; a French Jesuit's first article in *Études* on 1 Nov 1896; Mère Agnès asked Isidore on 6 Dec 1896 for "new documents"; *La Revue catholique de Coutances* said on 18 Dec 1896 Diana seemed fictitious; a Roman commission (Mgr Lazzareschi) was set up in Dec 1896; *Le Normand* (Lisieux) printed doubts on 30 Jan 1897. On Easter Monday 19 Apr 1897 Taxil told a full hall at the Société de Géographie, Paris, that he had invented Diana and called it the grandest hoax of his life. Only one image was shown, Thérèse's photo of Joan in prison, "from a Carmelite convent". *Le Normand* gave a notice on 21 Apr and a summary on 24 Apr. Per FR Wikipedia she learned on 21 Apr, tore up and burned Diana's letter, and tried to remove references from her writings. The Carmel suggests possible echoes (Céline to Br Siméon 25 Apr 1897; Ms C "there are truly souls who have no faith"; on that day Thérèse first called Bellière "my dear little brother", LT 224), but not as proof.
- When / where: summer 1896 – Apr 1897; Lisieux; Paris
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Isidore Guérin; Léo Taxil
- Source: Carmel chronology pages https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/derniere-annee-de-la-vie-de-therese/ and https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/oeuvres-de-therese/dernieres-paroles-de-therese/derniere-annee-de-la-vie-de-therese-2/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/personnage/taxil-leo-monsieur-ne-gabriel-antoine-jogand-pages/ ; FR and EN Wikipedia "Diana Vaughan", "Taxil hoax" (facts only)
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: unknown / facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for dates; medium on how she learned (CONFLICT 21 vs 24 Apr; see gaps).
- Notes: "About 400" in the audience appears only in a popular source (low confidence). Surprise: the hoax used her photo.
### F-LF-227 — The threat of persecution: Abbé Lechêne's sermon, 16 July 1896
- Claim: Thérèse wrote to Mme Guérin on 16 Jul 1896 that she believed she would soon "join the little angels", because Abbé Lechêne had preached in the Carmel chapel that day on the origin of the Order, compared the nuns to Elijah against the priests of Baal, and said "a period similar to that of Ahab's persecution was about to begin". "It seemed to us as though we were already hastening to martyrdom. What happiness, dear auntie, if all our family were to enter heaven on the same day." Laveille adds that when Marie de Gonzague told her of the coming persecution of religious communities she said to a companion, "Our Mother has just told me of the persecution already raging against religious communities… What joy! The good God is going to realize the grandest dream of my life" (Summarium 1919 p.715 §2166). In the same letter she said a doctor the day before said she "looked well".
- When / where: 16 Jul 1896; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mme Guérin; Abbé Lechêne; Marie de Gonzague
- Source: LAV p.261, p.455–456
- Source type: own-writing / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Historical context: F-LF-256 to F-LF-258 (anticlerical laws).
### F-LF-228 — The Hanoi plan and the novena to Théophane Vénard
- Claim: A French Carmel newly founded near Hanoi (Tonkin), itself founded from Saigon and so from Lisieux, asked Lisieux for nuns, and Marie de Gonzague "at one moment thought of sending Thérèse", though her health was already shaken. Thérèse's private notes: "I am loved here… For that reason I desire a monastery where I should be unknown, where I should have to suffer exile of heart… I would go to Hanoi to suffer much for God." Her health seemed to improve and the plan revived; in Nov 1896 she began a novena to Théophane Vénard. A grave relapse followed at once. The Carmel received a relic of Vénard on 6 Sep 1897; she had asked in vain several times, then stopped asking, and copied passages of his last letters for her sisters in Sep 1897.
- When / where: 1896–Sep 1897; Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Marie de Gonzague; Théophane Vénard
- Source: LAV p.262, p.371–372, p.390
- Source type: early-life / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Connection to other saints: Théophane Vénard (canonized 1988).
### F-LF-229 — The two missionary brothers
- Claim: Laveille says that Adolphe Roulland (then just ordained at the Foreign Missions Seminary, Paris) asked the Carmel, through his compatriot Fr Norbert of Mondaye, for a nun to pray for him; Marie de Gonzague chose Thérèse in 1896. He said Mass at the Carmel and spoke to her in the parlour. He went to Su-Tchuen (Sichuan), China, spent twelve years there, and was later procurator of the Society. Thérèse wrote him six letters; he was not the first: Abbé Maurice Bellière, aged twenty-one, seminarian of the White Fathers, was assigned to her by Mère Agnès. The letters began in Oct 1896 when Bellière, distressed by his coming departure, asked for prayers. His mother died a few days after his birth and he called his aunt Mme Barthélemy "mother". Thérèse never met him. In 1897 she wrote to him on 24 Feb, 21 Jun, 13 Jul, 25 Apr and 26 Jul (she called him "brother"). To Roulland she wrote 30 Jul 1896, 1 Nov 1896, 19 Mar 1897, 14 Jul 1897 and 14 Aug 1897.
- When / where: 1896–1897; Lisieux; Paris
- People: Adolphe Roulland; Maurice Bellière; Fr Norbert; Mère Agnès; Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.297–304, p.379–386
- Source type: early-life / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline names both)
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: Laveille calls Roulland "not the first missionary brother"; the order of assignments is as he says. Thérèse to Mère Agnès: correspondence with priests "may become a danger… should be rare" (Summarium 1919 p.562 §1553). The letter texts belong to the own-writings family.

### F-OM-012 — The brief says she was ill five months in bed and her bed became a "chair of holiness"
- Claim: The brief says that after her 23rd year she caught consumption of the lungs but did not relax the common rule until her strength failed; she lay "five months" in terrible pain with a cheerful face, and her sickbed was "a chair of holiness" for the whole community; she died on 30 Sep 1897 "refreshed by a heavenly vision".

- Translation: "the little bed of the handmaid of God, as she lay there, was a chair of holiness for the whole religious family"
- New to English readers: yes (wording)
- Confidence: high for the text
### F-OW-001 — Thérèse's own list of her "days of grace"
- Claim: At the end of Ms A, Thérèse wrote a list of the dates she counted as graces: birth 2 Jan 1873; baptism 4 Jan 1873; smile of the Virgin 13 May 1883 (she first wrote "1889" and the date was corrected); First Communion 8 May 1884; Confirmation 14 Jun 1884; conversion 25 Dec 1886; audience of Leo XIII 20 Nov 1887; entry to Carmel 9 Apr 1888; clothing 10 Jan 1889; "our great riches" 12 Feb 1889 (the day her father was taken to the Bon-Sauveur); canonical examination and blessing of Leo XIII Sept 1890; profession 8 Sep 1890; taking of the veil 24 Sep 1890; offering of herself to Love 9 Jun 1895.
- When / where: written 1895, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Thérèse

- Translation: "Our great riches, 12 February 1889."
- Other sources: The dates agree with 01-plan.md section 1.
- New to English readers: yes (the list and the phrase "our great riches" for her father's commitment are not in the baseline pages and are not in the 1911 edited text)

- Notes: She counts the day her father was committed to an asylum as "our great riches". The biography must explain this; it shows how she read suffering. The "1889" slip for the smile of the Virgin is hers; the Carmel corrects it to 1883.

### F-OW-193 — Profession delayed because the Superior found her too young; the wedding dress she must make alone
- Claim: To encourage Léonie, whose profession was delayed, Thérèse wrote that before her own profession she saw her novitiate year end and "no one was taking care of me (because of Our Father Superior, who found me too young)". "I assure you I was very sad." God showed her that in this desire there was "a great seeking of self". For the clothing she had been given "a beautiful white dress trimmed with lace and flowers". But who would give her one for her wedding? "That dress I must prepare all alone." Once she stopped worrying, Jesus united her to Himself sooner than she dared hope. She told Léonie that her wish to go to Le Mans was "a temptation".

### F-OW-194 — "Jesus's little toy": a toy of no value
- Claim: Thérèse reminded Léonie that she used to call herself "Jesus's little toy". She later thought that other souls with sublime virtues were His "beautiful toys", and she only "a little toy of no value". Children often have more fun with little toys they can "leave or take, break or kiss as they like". So she rejoiced to be poor. "Now that I have given you my direction, pray for me."

### F-OW-195 — The sister who "had the talent of displeasing me in everything"
- Claim: "There is in the community a sister who has the talent of displeasing me in everything; her manners, her words, her character seemed very disagreeable to me." Thérèse did for her what she would do for the person she loved most, prayed for her each time they met, gave her her "most lovable smile", and when the struggle was too hard "fled like a deserter". The sister once asked, pleased: "Would you tell me, Sr Thérèse of the Child Jesus, what attracts you so much to me? Each time you look at me I see you smile." Thérèse said she smiled because she was glad to see her ("I did not add that it was from the spiritual point of view").

### F-OW-200 — Léonie leaves the Visitation: the sisters' "agony" and the Uncle's calm
- Claim: The Martin sisters were "plunged in great grief because of our poor Léonie. It was like a true agony." God sent no consolation. Thérèse could only pray Christ's prayers: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken us!" and "Thy will be done". Then God sent "one of His Saints still travelling on earth": seeing her uncle's calm, their anguish disappeared. Her uncle's faith "can be compared to Abraham's". He seemed "already transfigured", blessing the hand that took "his Treasure" (Marie, entering the Carmel).
- When / where: 20 July 1895, Carmel parlour (the uncle's visit "yesterday")
- People: Isidore Guérin; Marie Guérin; Léonie; Mme Guérin

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « Nous étions plongées dans une bien grande douleur à cause de notre pauvre Léonie ; c'était comme une véritable agonie » ; « je ne pouvais faire une autre prière que celle de Notre Seigneur sur la croix : «Mon Dieu, mon Dieu, pourquoi nous avez-vous abandonnés!» »
- Translation: "We were plunged into very great grief because of our poor Léonie. It was like a real agony." / "I could say no other prayer than Our Lord's on the cross: 'My God, my God, why have you abandoned us!'"

### F-OW-201 — Léonie in the parlour, crying; Marie "a strong woman" who prefers "little angel"
- Claim: The next day Marie Guérin brought Léonie (just out of the Visitation) to the Carmel. "We could not get a word out of her, she was crying so much. At last she looked at us and all went well." Marie had been "a true strong woman". She disliked the compliment, so Thérèse called her "little angel", which she liked better. "She is gay enough to make stones laugh." The nuns served both girls "in earthenware dishes like the Carmelites, which amused them a lot". Marie wanted to give Thérèse "three sous for a stamp" rather than let her write.
- When / where: 21 July 1895, Carmel parlour
- People: Léonie; Marie Guérin; Mme Guérin

- Translation: "We could not get a word out of her, she was crying so hard. In the end she looked at us and everything went well." / "She is cheerful enough to make the stones laugh." / "We served them in earthenware dishes like the Carmelites, which amused them a lot."

### F-OW-203 — "The old dean of the novitiate" and the photographs at recreation
- Claim: Thérèse told Jeanne that the whole novitiate had caught Marie Guérin's gaiety. "The old dean of the novitiate", "despite my seven and a half years of religious life", often lost her gravity before "the charming Imp". "Our Mother" brought Jeanne's and Francis's photographs to recreation and passed them round. When Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie's turn came, she smiled at each, saying "Good morning, my Fifine… Good morning, my Seraph", and all the Carmelites laughed. Her "beautiful voice" was the charm of recreations.
- When / where: 14–17 Oct 1895, Carmel recreation
- People: Jeanne La Néele ("Fifine"); Francis La Néele ("mon Séraphin"); Sr Marie de l'Eucharistie (Marie Guérin), postulant; Mère Agnès ("Notre Mère")
- Source: LT 180 (to Mme La Néele, 14–15 and 17 Oct 1895), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-180-a-mme-la-neele-14-15-et-17-octobre-1895/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « C'est une grande consolation pour moi, la vieille doyenne du noviciat, de voir tant de gaîté entourer mes derniers jours » ; « «Bonjour, ma Fifine... Bonjour, mon Séraphin». Ces expressions de tendresse ont fait rire toutes les carmélites »
- Translation: "It is a great comfort to me, the old dean of the novitiate, to see so much gaiety around my last days." / "'Good morning, my Fifine… Good morning, my Seraph.' These words of tenderness made all the Carmelites laugh."

- New to English readers: yes ("vieille doyenne", "Bonjour, ma Fifine": ABSENT)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: "mes derniers jours" is said in jest, as she was 22. She was still in the novitiate after 7½ years.
### F-OW-204 — Flower patterns, not fish: "I have never liked what is eaten"
- Claim: Jeanne offered a feast present. Thérèse asked for "a flower pattern" (a model for painting) instead of fish. "My Uncle spoils his dear Carmelites, they are sure not to die of hunger… Little Thérèse, who has never liked what is eaten, does like things useful to her community." With patterns one can earn money to buy fish, "a bit like Perrette's story". She would like a branch of wild roses, or periwinkles, buttercups, "or even any other common flower". She added the pearls Jeanne had mentioned. "You see I am converted… I talk like a magpie and I am too bold in my requests."
- When / where: 14–17 Oct 1895
- People: Jeanne La Néele; Isidore Guérin
- Source: LT 180, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-180-a-mme-la-neele-14-15-et-17-octobre-1895/

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription: unknown
- Original words: « La petite Thérèse, qui n'a jamais aimé ce qui se mange, aime bien cependant les choses utiles à sa communauté, elle sait qu'avec des modèles, l'on peut gagner de l'argent pour acheter du poisson. » ; « je parle comme une pie »
- Translation: "Little Thérèse, who has never liked things to eat, does like things useful to her community. She knows that with patterns one can earn money to buy fish." / "I chatter like a magpie."

- New to English readers: yes (ABSENT in 1911)

- Notes: Her painting work helped the community's income. "Perrette" is La Fontaine's milkmaid who counts her profits before she has them.

### F-OW-210 — Her dreams: "woods, flowers, streams and the sea"
- Claim: "I attach no importance to my dreams... usually I dream of woods, flowers, streams and the sea, and almost always I see pretty little children, I catch butterflies and birds such as I have never seen." She wonders why, thinking of God all day, she does not dream of him more.

### F-OW-212 — Good Friday 1896: the first blood
- Claim: She had kept the Lenten fast "in all its rigour" and felt strong. On the night of Holy Thursday to Good Friday, after watching at the Repository until midnight, she lay down and "felt something like a stream rising, rising, bubbling up to my lips". The lamp was out; she waited for morning to confirm "my happiness", thinking it was blood. At dawn she went to the window and saw she was right. At the chapter of faults she told the prioress, adding she felt no pain and asking for no special treatment; she kept all the Good Friday austerities. The next night it happened again.
- When / where: night of 2–3 Apr 1896, and 3–4 Apr 1896; her cell, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Source: Ms C 4v–5r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je sentis comme un flot qui montait, montait en bouillonnant jusqu'à mes lèvres »
- Translation: "I felt something like a wave rising, rising, bubbling up to my lips"
- New to English readers: partial (the event is in baseline; her description is not)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She reports joy, not fear. She says she felt no pain.
### F-OW-213 — The trial of faith begins (Easter 1896): "a wall that rises to the heavens"
- Claim: During the Easter season 1896 "Jesus made me feel that there really are souls who do not have faith"; her soul was invaded "by the thickest darkness", and the thought of Heaven became "only a subject of combat and torment"; it had not ended by June 1897. She uses a parable of someone born in a land of fog who has heard of a sunny country; the darkness, "borrowing the voice of sinners", mocks her: "You dream of light... Go on, go on, rejoice in death, which will give you not what you hope for but a night deeper still, the night of nothingness." "I do not want to write more; I would be afraid of blaspheming." "It is no longer a veil for me, it is a wall that rises to the heavens and covers the starry sky." "When I sing of the happiness of Heaven... I feel no joy, for I simply sing what I WANT to believe." She says she has made more acts of faith in one year than in her whole life; she turns her back on the enemy "without deigning to look him in the face" and tells Jesus she is ready to shed her blood "to confess that there is a Heaven". She sits "at the table of sinners" and accepts to eat "the bread of sorrow" for them.
- When / where: Easter 1896 (Easter was 5 Apr 1896) to at least June 1897
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (recipient)
- Source: Ms C 5v–7v; 31r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « ce n'est plus un voile pour moi, c'est un mur qui s'élève jusqu'aux cieux et couvre le firmament étoilé »
- Translation: "it is no longer a veil for me; it is a wall that reaches to the heavens and hides the starry sky"
- Other sources: The 1898/1911 edited text keeps much of this passage but softens it (check wording in HA 1911 ch. IX). Her poems of 1896–1897 express joy; she says they do not show her state.
- New to English readers: partial (baseline names "trial of faith" only)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Essential, "not the pious picture" material. Ms A 80v (1891) already records a first doubt "whether there was a Heaven".
### F-OW-214 — Dream of Venerable Anne of Jesus, 10 May 1896
- Claim: Since Easter "the storm roared" in her soul. At dawn on Sunday 10 May 1896 she dreamed she was in a gallery with the prioress; three Carmelites in mantles and big veils came "from Heaven"; the tallest lifted her veil and covered Thérèse with it; she recognised Venerable Anne of Jesus, founder of Carmel in France, whom she had never prayed to. Her face "produced its own light". Thérèse asked: "Will God leave me long on earth? Will he come for me soon?" "Yes, soon, soon... I promise you." "Does God ask anything more of me than my poor little actions and desires? Is he pleased with me?" "God asks nothing else of you. He is pleased, very pleased!" She woke before she could ask graces for her sisters. "I believed, I felt that there is a Heaven."
- When / where: 10 May 1896, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Venerable Anne de Jésus (Ana de Lobera, 1545–1621)
- Source: Ms B 2r–2v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Oui, bientôt, bientôt... Je vous le promets. »
- Translation: "Yes, soon, soon... I promise you."
- Other sources: See M-OW-09.
- New to English readers: yes (not in baseline)
- Confidence: high as her report of a dream.

### F-OW-216 — 2 Aug 1896: Mère Agnès nearly sent to Saigon

### F-OW-217 — Ms B: "My vocation is love"
- Claim: Writing to Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur on the sixth anniversary of her profession (8 Sep 1896), she lists her impossible desires: to be warrior, priest, apostle, doctor, martyr; "the courage of a Crusader, of a Papal Zouave"; a priest, yet like Francis of Assisi refusing priesthood; missionary on five continents "from the creation of the world"; every martyrdom: flayed like Bartholomew, boiled like John, "like Joan of Arc, my dear sister, I would like to murmur your name at the stake". Reading 1 Corinthians 12–13 at prayer she found peace: "I understood that the Church had a Heart, and that this Heart was burning with love... My vocation, at last I have found it, my vocation is love!... In the heart of the Church, my Mother, I will be Love."
- When / where: 8 Sep 1896, retreat, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recipient)

- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-218 — Ms B: throwing flowers; the little bird who falls asleep
- Claim: Since she cannot preach or shed blood, she will "throw flowers": let no small sacrifice, look or word escape, doing the smallest things for love. When clouds hide the Sun, its little eyes close and it falls asleep, "still believing it is fixing its dear Star". She asks Jesus to choose "a legion of little victims".
